PREFACE 
 
 I, the Chairman of the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Home Affairs, 
having been authorized by the Committee to submit the report on its behalf, do hereby present this Two 
Hundred Fifty Second Report on Demands for Grants (2025-26) relating to the Ministry of Home 
Affairs (MHA). 
2. Under Rule 272 of the Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in the Council of States, the 
Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Home Affairs is mandated to consider the 
Demands for Grants of the related Ministries and make report thereon. In pursuance thereof, the 
Committee in its meetings held on 20th and 21st February, 2025 heard the Home Secretary and other 
officers and considered the Demands for Grants (2025-26) of the Ministry of Home Affairs.  
3. The Committee in its sitting held on 6th  March, 2025 considered and adopted the Report. 
4. The Committee while making its observations/recommendations has mainly relied upon the 
following documents:- 
 (i) Speech of Finance Minister on 1st February, 2025 while presenting the Union Budget 2025-
26; 
 (ii) Detailed Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Home Affairs for the year 2025-26; 
(iii) Output, Outcome, Monitoring framework of the Ministry 2025-26; 
(iv) Detailed Explanatory Notes on Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Home Affairs for the 
year 2025-26; 
(v) Written replies furnished by the Ministry to the Questionnaires sent to them by the 
Secretariat;  
(vi) Presentations made by the Home Secretary and other concerned officers;  
(vii) Written clarifications furnished by the Ministry, on the points/issues raised by the Members 
during the deliberations of the Committee; and 
(viii) Verbatim transcripts of the meetings of the Committee held on 20th  and 21st February, 
2025. 
 
5. For facility of reference and convenience, observations and recommendations of the Committee 
have been printed in bold letters in the body of the Report.

CHAPTER-I 
 
OVERVIEW 
 
1.1.1. The Ministry of Home Affairs (MHA) is responsible for internal security, border 
management, Centre-State relations, administration of Union Territories, management of Central 
Armed Police Forces (CAPFs), Disaster Management, etc. While discharging its duties, the Ministry 
of Home Affairs continuously monitors the internal security situation, issues appropriate advisories, 
shares intelligence inputs, extends manpower and financial support, guidance and expertise to the 
State Governments for maintenance of security, peace and harmony without encroaching upon the 
constitutional rights of the States.   Though 'Public Order' and 'Police' are the responsibilities of 
States as per the Seventh Schedule to the Constitution of India, Article 355 of the Constitution 
enjoins the Union to protect every State against external aggression and internal disturbance and to 
ensure that the Government of every State is carried on in accordance with the provisions of the 
Constitution.  
 
1.2 Goals  and  Objectives 
 
1.2.1 The Outcome Budget (2025-26) of the Ministry of Home Affairs throws light on its goals and 
objectives. The MHA administers and implements a wide arch of schemes and programmes as 
mentioned therein in detail.  In brief, the goals and objectives of the Ministry for the year (2025-26) 
are as follows:- 
 
(i) Continuous monitoring of  internal security situation and issuing timely 
advisories; 
(ii) Implementing a zero-tolerance policy against terrorism and drug trafficking; 
(iii) Strengthening border management strategies to enhance national security; 
(iv) Promoting the modernization of the criminal justice system through new laws and 
digitization; 
(v) Enhancing forensic science capabilities to improve the quality of investigations; 
(vi) Developing an Inter-operable Criminal Justice System (ICJS) to facilitate 
seamless  communication between law enforcement agencies; 
(vii) Supporting disaster management initiatives to enhance preparedness and response 
capabilities; 
(viii) Fostering community engagement programs to promote peace, harmony and 
social cohesion; 
(ix) Implementing initiatives for women's safety and empowerment to ensure a secure 
environment for women; 
(x) Timely disbursement of funds for freedom fighters and their families; 
(xi) Ensuring the provision of security, administrative and residential infrastructure of 
CAPFs; 
(xii) Ensuring security and administrative infrastructure for Central Forensic Science 
Laboratories (CFSLs); 
(xiii) Enhancing training, capacity-building and skill development programs for police 
personnel to improve their skills and efficiency; 
(xiv) Supporting the holistic development of islands in Union Territories to enhance 
local governance and infrastructure; 

2 
 
 
(xv) Conducting regular census and surveys to maintain accurate demographic data 
and support policy formulation; 
(xvi) Reimbursement of expenditure under Security Related Expenditure (SRE) to 
States; 
(xvii) Special Infrastructure Support for Left Wing Extremism (LWE) affected States; 
(xviii) Fostering international cooperation on security issues, including counter-terrorism 
and transnational crime; 
(xix) Establishment of a Bhartiya Bhasha Anubhag to support translation services and 
promote linguistic diversity; 
(xx) Scheme for support to poor prisoners; 
(xxi) Modernization of prisons; and 
(xxii) Streamlining immigration processes through the Immigration, Visa, 
Foreigners Registration and Tracking (IVFRT) system.

CHAPTER-II 
 
OVERALL ASSESSMENT OF DEMANDS FOR GRANTS (2025-26) OF THE MHA 
 
2.1 OVERVIEW 
 
2.1.1 There are eleven Demands for Grants which are administered by the Ministry of Home 
Affairs (MHA). The detailed Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Home Affairs are divided into 
three volumes: Vol. I, Vol. II-A and Vol. II-B. The Detailed Demands for Grants (Volume-I) 
contains three Grants (Nos. 49-51), namely the Ministry of Home Affairs (Grant No. 49); the 
Cabinet (Grant No. 50); and the Police (Grant No. 51). 
 
2.1.2. The Detailed Demands for Grants (Volumes-II-A and II-B) relate to the five Union Territories 
(without Legislature), namely Andaman & Nicobar Islands (Grant No. 52), Chandigarh (Grant 
No.53), Dadra & Nagar Haveli and Daman & Diu (Grant No. 54), Ladakh (Grant No. 55) and 
Lakshadweep (Grant No. 56), and three Union Territories (with Legislature), namely Delhi (Grant 
No. 57- Transfers to Delhi), Jammu & Kashmir (Grant No.58- Transfers to Jammu & Kashmir) and 
Puducherry (Grant No. 59- Transfers to Puducherry). 
 
2.1.3 The provisions for the Union Territories (without Legislature) form part of the Demands of 
the Ministry of Home Affairs. However, various programmes/schemes are the subject matter of 
different Ministries, which are responsible for scrutinizing, sanctioning and implementing the 
schemes/projects falling under their respective purview. Provisions for the Union Territories (with a 
Legislature) cover Assistance to the Union Territories of Puducherry, Jammu & Kashmir and NCT 
of Delhi, in the form of the Grants and Loans. In respect of these three Grants, the Ministry of Home 
Affairs releases the Grants on quarterly basis and has a limited role in scrutinizing, sanctioning and 
implementation of the schemes/projects.

2.2.2 It can be seen from the table 2.1 above that the allocation made to the MHA (Demand No. 49) 
for BE 2025-26 is ₹ 4557.19 crores which is a reduction of 29.44%from the allocation of ₹ 6458.24 
crores in BE 2024-25.The allocation made to the MHA (Demand No. 51) for BE 2025-26 is 
₹1,60,391.06 crores which shows an increase of 11.95 % from the allocation of ₹1,43,275.90 crores 
made in BE 2024-25.   
 
2.2.3 The Committee notes that the allocation made to the MHA (Demand No. 52-59 Union 
Territories) for BE 2025-26 is ₹67,238.13 which is 28.83% of the total allocation made to MHA in BE 
2025-26. This is a decrease of 2.07 % from the allocation of ₹ 68660.26 crores in BE 2024-25.  The 
combined allocation made for 5 Union Territories (Without Legislature) for BE 2025-26 is ₹21457.85 
crores which is a decrease of 2.2% from the allocation of ₹21945.51 crores in BE 2024-25. The 
combined allocation made for 3 Union Territories (With Legislature) for BE 2025-26 is ₹45780.28 
crores which is a decrease of 2.0 % from the allocation of ₹46714.75 crores in BE 2024-25. 
 
2.2.4 For 2025-26, the total allocation for the MHA for BE 2025-26 is ₹ 2,33,210.68 croreswhich is 
an increase of 6.17% from the allocation of ₹ 2,19,643.31 crores in BE 2024-25. The total allocation 
granted for the MHA at BE stage for past 3 three years i.e. 2022-23, 2023-24 and 2024-25 was 
₹1,85,776.55 crores, ₹1,96,034.94 crores and ₹2,19,643.31 crores respectively. This shows the 
increasing trend in budgetary allocation made to the MHA. The Committee expresses its satisfaction 

6 
 
that considering the important functions administered by the Ministry of Home Affairs, the Budget of 
the Ministry has been subsequently increased every year including this year.

2.5.2 The above table 2.5 indicates that the actual Budget Estimates (BE) for 2025-26 for various 
police-related expenditures are lower than the projected amounts for BE 2025-26. Notable reductions 
are observed in the budgets for the Central Armed Police Forces (CAPFs), Central Police 
Organisations (CPOs), the Modernisation of State Police Forces and Criminal Tracking Network and 
Systems (CCTNS), the upgradation of Central Forensic Science Laboratories, Intelligence Bureau, 
Delhi Police and many other programs.  
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
2.5.3 The Committee observes, based on the above-mentioned information, that the actual 
Budget Estimate (BE) for 2025-26 is lower than the projected BE for 2025-26 across several 
key areas, reflecting a more conservative allocation than initially anticipated. The total budget 
has been reduced from ₹184,236.94 crores (projected BE 2025-26) to ₹160391.06 crores (actual 
BE 2025-26), marking a 12.94% decrease. Within the Central Armed Police Forces (CAPFs), 
actual allocations fall short of projections, with reductions observed across the CRPF, NSG, 
BSF, ITBP, CISF, Assam Rifles, and SSB. This indicates a recalibration of expenditure 
priorities. Similarly, significant downward revisions have been made to allocations for the 
Intelligence Bureau (IB), NATGRID, Central Police Organizations (CPOs) and Delhi Police. 
Police infrastructure projects, particularly CAPF building projects, have experienced a 

11 
 
substantial reduction from ₹6,403.02 crores to ₹4,038.70 crores, which may potentially impact 
construction of new buildings. The Committee notes that such reductions may adversely affect 
critical schemes under these heads and may slow down modernization programs as well as 
hinder the execution of developmental projects, particularly in border areas. Hence, the 
Committee recommends that the MHA may take up the matter with the Ministry of Finance to 
seek increased allocations under these heads in alignment with their original projections. This 
will ensure that the essential developmental schemes are not affected due to insufficient 
funding. The Committee hopes that funds may be demanded for various developmental and 
expansion projects at the RE stage depending on the utilization pattern. 
 
2.5.4 The Committee observes that the significant reduction in the Grant-in-Aid to States for 
Home Guards from ₹ 566.01 crores in RE 2024-25 to ₹30.01 crores in BE 2025-26 is based on 
the expectation that all backlog claims will be cleared in FY 2024-25. However, the Committee 
emphasizes the need to reassess the funding requirements for new claims and ensure that 
States are adequately supported to maintain the operational efficiency of Home Guards. 
Similarly, with respect to the Holistic Development of Islands in Union Territories, where the 
allocation has been reduced from ₹ 88 crores in RE 2024-25 to a nominal ₹0.01 crore in BE 
2025-26, the Committee recommends expediting the preparation of EFC papers and project 
reports to ensure timely approval and funding for developmental activities in the UTs. The 
Committee further recommends closely monitoring expenditure trends and engaging with 
stakeholders to ensure that reductions across various components do not adversely impact 
service delivery or developmental goals. 
 
2.5.5 The Committee recommends the Ministry to take effective measures to address this 
issue by setting clear quarterly targets for fund utilization, enhancing planning and monitoring 
mechanisms to track progress and identifying bottlenecks in project execution at an early 
stage. The Ministry should also consider establishing a reporting mechanism to monitor 
monthly fund utilization and take corrective action wherever delays are identified. The 
Committee is of the view that by adopting these measures, the Ministry can ensure that 
allocated funds are utilized efficiently and effectively throughout the year, thereby avoiding 
last-minute spending rushes and ensuring that the major projects are executed as planned 
without any delay.

Observation/ Recommendation 
 
2.6.6 In the meeting of the Committee held on 21st February, 2025, it was informed by the 
officials of the MHA  that the total expenditure incurred by all UTs under Demand No. 57-59 
(UTs with Legislature) against the allocation made in RE 2024-25, has been 90.91%.  The UT 
of Delhi, J&K  and Puducherry have spent 85.42%, 89.16% and 98.17% respectively, against 
the transfer made by the MHA to them during RE 2024-25.  
 
2.6.7 The Committee notes that there has been a decrease of 2.07% in allocations made for 
the Union Territories (UTs) in the year 2025-26 as compared to the year 2024-25. Further, 
there has been no substantial increase in the capital budget for the UTs (without legislatures) 
during thepast three years. In fact, it has been slightly reduced during the Financial Year 
2025-26 as compared to Financial Year 2024-25. The Committee is of the view that to meet the 
development needs of UTs, adequate capital fund is required so that the projects pertaining to 
the development of physical and social infrastructure like roads, power, water and sanitation, 
health and education in all UTs do not suffer due to lack of sufficient funds. Further, to ensure 
that the sensitive and fragile ecology of some of the Union Territories are not affected, special 
measures will have to be taken while implementing the developmental projects. This requires 
extra expenditure which in turn demands more allocation of funds. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry may try to address the needs of the Union Territories and any 
shortfall in capital budget may be provided at RE stage so that they could continue with their 
ongoing projects and schemes and initiate new schemes.

CHAPTER III 
 
DEMAND NO. 49 - MINISTRY OF HOME AFFAIRS 
 
3.1 OVERVIEW 
 
3.1.1 The Demand No. 49 of the Ministry of Home Affairs (MHA) contains provisions mainly for 
various administrative functions in respect of MHA Secretariat, Office of Registrar General and 
Census Commissioner of India, various offices functioning under Department of Official Language, 
National Disaster Response Force, National Disaster Management Authority, Home Guards, Civil 
Defence, National Human Rights Commission, Fire Services and Departmental Accounting 
Organisation of MHA.  
 
3.1.2 Apart from the above, provisions have been made for schemes like Relief & Rehabilitation 
for repatriates and migrants, Pension and other benefits to Freedom Fighters, Helicopter subsidies for 
North-East, Jammu & Kashmir, Ladakh and Himachal Pradesh and various schemes of Disaster 
Management. 
  
3.2 OFFICIAL LANGUAGE 
 
3.2.1 The allocations under Official Language include provisions for Central Hindi Training 
Institute, Central Translation Bureau, Technical Cell, Regional Implementation Offices and other 
schemes / programmes being implemented by the Department of Official Language.  
 
3.2.2  The Committee sought to know about the measures that have been worked out for the 
promotion of official language. The MHA informed about the efforts made by the Department of 
Official Language, which are as follows:- 
(i) Since 2020, a series of All India Official Language conferences have been organized to 
promote Hindi and implement the Official Language Policy effectively. Awards such as 
the Rajbhasha Kirti Award were given to entities excelling in Hindi implementation, while 
individuals contributing to Hindi promotion receive Rajbhasha Gaurav Awards. 
(ii) Regional Official Language Conferences are organized four times a year to address 
challenges and recognize Hindi usage at the regional level.  
(iii) Developed Kanthastha Software with C-DAC Pune, this AI-based software assists in 
translating between English to Hindi and vice versa. It has been integrated into e-office 
systems and is available as a mobile app, enhancing accessibility for users.  
(iv) Establishment of Bhartiya Bhasha Anubhag aiming to strengthen communication between 
the Central Government and State Governments in their respective official languages. The 
project includes developing a universal translation system in 15 Indian languages 
recognized in the Eighth Schedule of the Constitution.  
(v) Over 500 Town Official Language Implementation Committees (TOLICs) operate across 
the country to enhance Hindi usage in Central Government offices, undertakings and 
banks. Additionally, five international TOLICs have been established in Port Louis, Dubai, 
London, Fiji and Singapore to promote Hindi abroad.  

15 
 
(vi) The Kendriya Hindi Samiti, headed by the Prime Minister, reviews Hindi's implementation 
status in Ministries and Departments. The Central Official Language Implementation 
Committee (COLIC) headed by the Secretary of the Department of Official Language, 
meets annually to discuss progress and strategies for enhancing Hindi usage.The 
Committee in its meeting held on 20th February, 2025, while discussing DFG (2025-26) of 
the MHA discussed the efforts of Department of Official Language and appreciated the 
efforts taken by the Ministry to spread the usage of Hindi.  
 
3.2.4 From the information provided by the MHA, it may be seen that while the allocation in  RE 
of 2024-25 was ₹ 79.71 crores, in BE 2025-26 an allocation of ₹ 95.84 crores has been made under 
the head ‘Official language’. Thus, there has been an increase of ₹ 16.13 crores over the past year’s 
allocation.  
Observation/ Recommendation 
3.2.5 The Committee appreciates the Ministry's initiatives, such as All India and Regional 
Official Language Conferences, for significantly advancing the use of Hindi and implementing 
the Official Language Policy effectively. The Committee is of the view that to further 
strengthen these efforts, the Ministry could consider increasing the frequency and regional 
outreach of these conferences, incorporating interactive workshops and addressing practical 
challenges faced by Government offices. The Committee commends the development of the AI-
based translation software Kanthastha, noting that its scope could be expanded by integrating 
additional Indian languages and enhancing technical translation accuracy. Further, the 
Committee recognizes the establishment of Bhartiya Bhasha Anubhag, as it aims to facilitate 
official communication between the Central and State Governments in their respective official 
languages, ensuring linguistic inclusivity in governance. The Committee appreciates that the 
Budget under the head “official language” has been increased to the tune of more than 20% 
which may enablethe Ministry toprioritize engaging technical expertise and ensuring seamless 
integration with Government communication systems. 
 
3.3 NATIONAL DISASTER RESPONSE FORCE (NDRF) 
 
3.3.1 The Committee in its meeting held on 20th  February, 2025, while discussing DFG (2025-26) 
for the MHA appreciated the efforts of NDRF in rescue and rehabilitation during disasters. It has 
been informed to the Committee that as of now, NDRF has astrength of 16 battalions, consisting of 
more than 18000 personnel, which are stationed in different parts of the country based on the 
vulnerability profile. The force has emerged as a visible, vibrant, multi-skilled, hi-tech and 
standalone forcecapable of dealing with all types of natural and man-made disasters, including 
Chemical, Biological, Radiological, and Nuclear (CBRN) emergencies. The locations of the 16 
battalions are at Jammu & Kashmir; Ghaziabad (Uttar Pradesh), Patna (Bihar), Guwahati (Assam), 
Vadodara (Gujarat), Pune (Maharashtra), Arakkonam (Tamil Nadu), Vijayawada (Andhra Pradesh), 
Mundali (Odisha), Haringhata (West Bengal), Varanasi (Uttar Pradesh), Hollongi (Arunachal 
Pradesh), Ladhowal (Punjab), Nurpur (Himachal Pradesh), Gadarpur (Uttarakhand) and Dwarka 
(New Delhi). In addition, teams of NDRF have also been stationed at 28 different strategic 
locationsto reduce the response time in case of disasters. The Ministry also apprised the Committee 
that NDRF is providing training and allocating funds to State Disaster Relief Force (SDRF) to 
strengthen disaster responseall across the country. 

16 
 
 
3.3.2 The Committee was informed that during the period from 01.01.2023 to 31.03.2024, NDRF 
teams conducted various operations and saved 57,651 precious lives, including 6,812 rescued 
persons and 50,839 evacuated persons.  Additionally, 2,754 livestock were saved, and 779 dead 
bodies were retrieved. The teams alsoprovided medical assistance/PHT to 15,045 people. Further, 
NDRF teams assisted the local administration of the States and Union Territories of Andhra Pradesh, 
Assam, Bihar, Chandigarh, Dadra & Nagar Haveli and Daman &Diu, Delhi, Gujarat, Haryana, 
Himachal Pradesh, Jammu and Kashmir, Kerala, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, Odisha, Punjab, 
Rajasthan, Telangana, Uttar Pradesh, Uttarakhand, and West Bengal for the rescue and evacuation of 
affected people during floods. 
 
3.3.3 The provision allotted for National Disaster Response Force (NDRF) under Revenue Section 
is to meet its administrative expenditure and establishment cost.  The provision under Capital 
Section is for procurement of Machinery & Equipment and Motor Vehicles of the Force. The 
Committee has been informed by the Ministry about the allocation of NDRF from ₹ 1606.95 crores 
in BE 2024-25 to ₹1922.59 crores in BE2025-26. 
 
3.3.4 During the meeting of the Committee, on a query regarding Aapda Mitra Scheme, the 
Ministry has informed about the target of training of 100,000 able-bodied volunteers (Aapda Mitra) 
in disaster response for floods, cyclone, landslide and earthquake relief and rescue operations in 350 
disaster prone districts in the country. This will help protect communities living in disaster prone 
areas. 
3.3.5 The disaster response training focuses on enhancing preparedness, technical skills, and team 
management for responders before, during and after disasters. The training incorporates lessons 
learned from past disaster responses to improve efficiency. The key highlights of the training from 
01.01.2023 to 31.03.2024 are as follows:- 
(i)  In-house training was conducted at NDRF Academy, Nagpur for 1,253 personnel andat 
unit levels for 11,960 personnel. 
(ii)  External training included specialized courses such as Aquatic Disaster Response for 675 
personnel, Heli Slithering for 260 personnel, Industrial Chemical Emergency for60 
personnel and Forest Fire Response for 150 personnel. 
(iii)  Training for stakeholders included SDRF training for 2,257 personnel, disaster
 management training for 245 CAPF personnel and training for 19,517 students from 
Jawahar Navodaya Vidyalaya. 
(iv)  Community awareness and school safety programs included 1,984 community awareness 
programs benefiting 3,26,051 people and 2,361 school safety programsreaching 7,77,017 
students. 
(v)  Mock exercises were conducted across different sectors with 844 drills benefiting 2,44,547 
personnel. This included 381 drills with PSUs and MAH units covering 80,984 personnel, 
63 drills with Indian Railways covering 15,852 personnel and 400 district-level exercises 
benefiting 1,47,711 personnel. 
 
 
 
 

17 
 
 
The NDMA has been advocating the cause of sustainable development through its various 
guidelines/studies and workshops. These guidelines are as follows:- 
(i) Management of Earthquakes (2007) 
(ii) Guidelines on Management of  Landslides and snow Avalanches (2009) 
(iii) Seismic Retro fitting of Deficient Buildings (2014) 
(iv) Simplified guidelines on Earthquake (2021) 
(v) Landslide Risk Management Strategy (2019) 
(vi) Guidelines on Management of Glacial Lake Outburst Floods (GLOFs) (2020) 
(vii)  Compendium of Task Force Report on NDMA Guidelines on Management of Glacial 
Lake Outburst Floods (GLOF's) (2020) 
(viii) Summary for Policy Makers on NDMA Guidelines on Management of GLOFs (2020) 
 
3.3.6 During the course of the meetings, the Committee desired to be apprised about the disaster 
management strategy that needs to be adopted in the Himalayan region.  The MHA informed that 
given the terrain and challenges in accessibility and communication, local level response capacity 
including community-based disaster management needs to be further enhanced. There is a need for 
greater investment in ensuring the disaster resilience of all critical infrastructures Geological Survey 
of India (GSI) has undertaken land slide susceptibility mapping of the hill regions.  These are being 
further developed at a higherresolution.Local administrations are being encouraged to use thesemaps 
fordevelopmentplanning. 
3.3.7 It has been informed that thestatus of installations of early warning system for disasters is as 
follows:- 
 Earthquake: Ministry of Earth Science (MoES) is the nodal Ministry for earthquakes. 
National Center for Seismology (NCS) is the nodal agency of the Government ofIndia for 
monitoring of earthquake activity in the country. NCS maintains National Seismological 
Network of more than 150 stations each having state of art equipmentand spreading all across 
the country. 
 
 Weather related hazards: For various weather related hazards such as cyclones, rainfall, 
cloudburst, thunder storms and lightening, heat wave and cold waves, Indian Meteorological 
Department (IMD) (Under MoES) issues early warning. It operates its network of weather 
instruments spread across the country. 
 
 Landslide: The Ministry of Mines is the nodal Ministry for land slide related issues. 
Geological Survey of India (GSI) has undertaken landslide susceptibility mapping ofthe hill 
regions. These are being further developed at a higher resolution. NDMA has also 
implemented projection “Development of Low-Cost Landslide Monitoring Solutions in 
collaboration with IIT Mandi in which low cost early warning system has been developed 
which is quite cheaper to commercial available systems. The samekind of system will be 
scaled up under the forthcoming National Programme on Landslide Risk Reduction and 
Mitigation. 

18 
 
 
 Glacier Lake Outburst Flood (GLOF): The Central Water Commission and Ministry of Jal 
Shakti are the nodal agency.  NDMA has also issued the guidelines on GLOF(2020). 
 Common Alerting Protocol (CAP): Integrated Alert System (Sachet) Phase-I is being 
implemented in all 36 States/UTs under which Alert Generating Agencies and Alert 
Dissemination Agencies have been integrated and access provided to them with CAP for alert 
dissemination through SMS for landslides, cloud bursts and avalanches, etc. 
 
3.3.8 Besides, NDMA has issued National Disaster management Plan (NDMP in 2016, which was 
further revised in 2019. Further all the State Governments in the Himalayan regions have prepared 
Disaster Management Plans at State and District levels, under the provisions of the Disaster 
Management Act 2005. 
3.3.9 Further, on a query made by the Committee in the meeting about the vacancy rate in NDRF, 
the Ministry has replied that NDRF is 100% deputation’s force. Hence, CAPFs are forwarding 
nominations of personnel to NDRF headquarter (HQ) for concurrence who meet the requisite 
qualitative requirements (QRs) before their induction in NDRF Battalion on deputation for seven 
years. Presently, NDRF is having overall 21% vacancies. At present, almost all the CAPFs are 
carrying the same number of vacancies. However, CAPFs are being approached at regular interval to 
fill up the existing vacancies of NDRF. 
OBSERVATION/RECOMMENDATION 
 
3.3.10 The Ministry of Home Affairs (MHA) informed the Committee that for FY 2025-26, the  
NDRF has been allocated ₹1,922.59 crores against a projected requirement of ₹2,098.63 crores, 
resulting in a shortfall of ₹176.04 crores. The Committee notes this gap and hopes it will not 
adversely impact the functioning of the NDRF. It appreciates the critical role of the NDRF in 
disaster response and emphasizes the need for adequate funding to address increasing 
demands, particularly for additional personnel. The Committee recommends that the MHA 
may take up the matter with the Ministry of Finance at the Revised Estimates (RE) stage to 
secure the remaining funds, ensuring that operational efficiency and preparedness are not 
compromised during emergencies. 
 
3.3.11 The Committee notes the commendable efforts in launching the Yuva Aapda Mitra 
Scheme to train young volunteers for disaster response and recognizes its potential to 
strengthen community resilience nationwide. The Committee recommends developing 
standardized training modules that comprehensively cover essential disaster response skills, 
ensuring uniformity and effectiveness in community disaster preparedness including local 
committees, youths and youth organisation.  Additionally, the Committee emphasizes the need 
for regular monitoring and evaluation of program effectiveness to identify areas for 
improvement and ensure that the approved financial outlay is efficiently allocated to support 
training and resource procurement. Further, the Committee recommends promoting the 
creation of Community Emergency Stockpiles to enhance disaster preparedness. 
 
3.3.12 The Committee emphasizes the need for the Ministry to conduct comprehensive 
research to develop a long-term roadmap for disaster resilience and adaptation, focusing 
onfuture requirements for mitigation and preparedness in light of climate change and 
increasing disaster frequency. The Committee urges the Ministry to increase investment in 

19 
 
disaster-resilient infrastructure, including critical facilities such as hospitals, schools and 
transport networks, to minimize the impact of disasters and improve recovery efforts. The 
Committee recommends that the Ministry may include new and emerging disasters in its 
disaster management plans, such as those caused by heat waves etc.  It further recommends 
establishing a formal mechanism for the periodic review and update of the Notified Disasters 
list to ensure the Act remains relevant and responsive to emerging disaster risks through 
consultation with experts, stakeholders and affected communities. 
 
3.3.13 Acknowledging that the NDRF is entirely dependent on deputation from CAPFs and 
currently faces a 21% vacancy rate, while CAPFs themselves are experiencing staffing 
shortages, the Committee recommends the Ministry to conduct a thorough review of the 
deputation policies. The Committee is of the view that the review should assess the impact of 
the current seven-year deputation tenure and explore incentives, such as flexible terms or 
enhanced allowances, to encourage more CAPF personnel to volunteer for NDRF assignments. 
Streamlining the nomination process and improving communication between NDRF and 
CAPFs are also crucial steps for expediting the filling of vacancies and ensuring the NDRF's 
operational readiness.

appointments made, with the remaining in process) and constructing 6,000 transit accommodations 
(3,120 completed, with the rest under construction). Special awareness drives are underway to 
promote self-employment schemes, PM Kisaan Saman Nidhi Schemes, Social Welfare Schemes and 
the Ayushman Bharat Scheme, with a focus on skill development, self-employment, social 
assistance, studies and sports, with over 500 youth receiving financial support for businesses. 
Migrant ration cards are being integrated into the National Food Security Act (NFSA) database 
following amendments to Jammu & Kashmir Food Security Rules, 2021 and the Targeted Public 
Distribution (Control) Order 2023, ensuring migrants can access Government schemes. The West 
Pakistan Refugees received one-time financial aid, with ₹14.78 crores disbursed this year. For Bru 
migrants, as per the 2020 agreement, each family is entitled to a Fixed Deposit of ₹4 Lakhs, cash 
assistance of ₹5000 per month, free ration for 2 years, House Building Assistance of ₹1,50,000, a 
plot of land and free transportation. Resettlement in Tripura is progressing, with 6,935 families 
shifted to 12 locations, construction of 6,376 houses completed and financial assistance disbursed. 
The Government of Tripura is providing skill development and livelihood training to the Bru 
community.

recommends enhancing awareness drives to ensure all migrant and refugee groups are fully 
aware of and can access various Government schemes, such as self-employment programs, 
social welfare initiatives and healthcare benefits and streamlining the online platform for 
issuing essential certificates.

3.6 HELICOPTOR SERVICES  
3.6.1 The Ministry has informed the Committee that the scheme provides subsidized helicopter 
services in the North Eastern Region, Ladakh, Jammu & Kashmir and Himachal Pradesh to facilitate 
access to remote areas. The budget allocation for the helicopter services scheme has seen a change in 
actual BE for 2024-25 and 2025-26. The revenue allocation for 2023-24 was ₹101.92 crores, with 
the Budget Estimates (BE) for 2024-25 set at ₹115.00 crores, but the Revised Estimates (RE) for 
2024-25 decreased to ₹99.00 crores, which is also the BE for 2025-26. The Ministry has informed 
that the variation between the RE for 2024-25 and the BE for the same year indicates a decrease due 
to lesser demand from State/UT Governments. Similarly, the provision for BE 2025-26 has been 
reduced based on demand trends, with any additional requests to be addressed during the RE process 
for that year. It was also informed that within the allotted flying hours, State Governments decide the 
routes/sectors as per their requirements. During FY 2024-25, helicopter services were 
operationalized in the new sector in Manipur for Imphal- Ukhrul- Imphal. 
Observation/Recommendation 
3.6.2  The Committee notes the reduction in budget allocation for helicopter services due to 
decreased demand from State and UT Governments and acknowledges the critical role these 
services play in providing accessibility to remote areas. It appreciates the operationalization of 
helicopter services in new sectors like Imphal-Ukhrul-Imphal in Manipur. The Committee 
recommends the Ministry to explore the possibility of airstrips in strategic locations as a 
sustainable and reliable alternative to helicopter services for long-term feasibility, economic 
growth and social cohesion in remote regions.The Committee suggests that the Ministry of 
Home Affairs may conduct a survey to identify the abandoned air strips in the North Eastern 
Region which were functional before the independence.  Revival of such air strips may enhance 
connectivity to remote population of the country. 
3.7  INFRASTRUCTURE  FOR DISASTER  MANAGEMENT

building specialized disaster response capabilities. The capital budget allocation for infrastructure of 
disaster management shows a fluctuating trend.The BE for 2024-25 was initially set at ₹152.00 
crores but was later revised to ₹81.67 crores. The Budget Estimate for 2025-26 is ₹100.00 crores. 
The decrease in the RE for 2024-25 compared to the BE is due to reduced demand for buildings and 
structures by the National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA). The further decrease in the BE 
for 2025-26 is attributed to reduced land requirements for the NDRF. The actual utilization of funds 
was ₹84.38 crores in 2023-24 and ₹59.58 crores have been utilized until 31stJanuary, 2025 in FY 
2024-25. 

23 
 
Observation/Recommendation

preparedness. 
 3.8 CENSUS, SURVEY AND  STATISTICS/REGISTRAR  GENERAL OF INDIA 
3.8.1. The Ministry while giving its presentation to the Committee has mentioned about the 
allocation of ₹1,309.46 crores in BE 2024-25 under this head in anticipation of declaration of Census 
and updation of National Population Register (NPR) which got postponed earlier due to outbreak of 
Corona. As census was not declared, the RE for 2024-25 was reduced to ₹572 crores. In view of this,

for various activities under this Head. The Ministry has further informed that many of the Census 
related preparatory activities have been completed and technological updation is in progress. 
Therefore, as and when Census exercise will be undertaken, additional funds will be sought as per 
the year-wise requirement. 
3.8.2 It has been informed that the proposed expenditure for FY 2025-26 encompasses various 
activities aimed at continuing preparatory work for the Census and NPR, including technological 
updates. It covers the salaries and allowances for officers and officials involved in Census, NPR, 
Civil Registration System (CRS), Sample Registration System (SRS), geospatial mapping, IT and 
language departments. Additionally, the expenditure will be directed towards the operation and 
maintenance of the National Register of Citizens(NRC) data centre and security measures for the 
NRC in Assam. 
3.8.3 Significant progress was made in the development of geospatial databases for the Census, 
including the harmonization of administrative boundaries in collaboration with Survey of India and 
the creation of a centralized Census Mapping Portal for data management. The National Data Centre 
and Disaster Recovery sites were also operationalized to support data storage and disaster 
management.  
3.8.4 Further, the Civil Registration Systems (CRS) saw updates with the amendment of the 
Registration of Births and Deaths Act, 1969, and the launch of a revamped CRS portal to facilitate 
online registration and issuance of birth and death certificates in 24 States/UTs. For the Sample 
Registration System (SRS), activities included the automation of data collection and processing, the 
conduct of part-time enumerator training and the deployment of role-based user access for data 

24 
 
security. The development of an improved web portal for monitoring fieldwork and data aggregation 
in real-time also contributed to the efficiency of these systems. 
Observation/Recommendation

National Population Register (NPR) and appreciates the continuous efforts in enhancing the 
technological infrastructure and data management systems, particularly the development of

System (CRS) portal.The Committee notes the assurance given by the Ministry that, if

3.8.6 The Committee further appreciates the timely implementation of the amended 
Registration of Births and Deaths Act, 1969 and the successful launch of the revamped CRS 
portal in multiple States/UTs, which will improve accessibility and efficiency in birth and 
death registrations. The automation of the Sample Registration System (SRS) and the 
deployment of improved web portals are commendable steps towards streamlining data 
collection and real-time monitoring. The Committeefeels that the Ministry may explore and 
leverage additional existing digital platforms and tools, such as mobile phone connections, 
Aadhaar and other Government data bases for real-time data collection.  Further, linking the 
data collected through these digital channels with the Aadhaar database can provide 
additional verification layers, ensuring data integrity and reducing the need for manual cross-
checking. 
3.9 NATIONAL CYCLONE RISK MITIGATION PROJECT (NCRMP) 
 
3.9.1 The provision under National Cyclone Risk Mitigation Project (NCRMP) is for 
administrative expenditure and Grants-in-Aid to States with World Bank Assistance to minimize the 
vulnerability in the cyclone hazard prone coastal states. The budgetary allocation under this head is 
given below:- 
Table 3.5 
Fund allotted under National Cyclone Risk Mitigation Project (NCRMP) 
 
 Actuals 2023-24 BE 2024-25 RE 2024-25 BE 2025-26 
Revenue 0.00 10.87 4.33 0.00 
 
3.9.2 As per the Annual report for the Ministry 2023-24, the Government of India approved the 
National Cyclone Risk Mitigation Project (NCRMP) with the overall objective of minimizing 
vulnerability to cyclones and making people and infrastructure disaster-resilient while conserving the 
coastal ecosystem in cyclone hazard-prone States/Union Territories of India. The project had four 
components namely: i) Component A: Early Warning Dissemination System ensuring last mile 
connectivity ii) Component B: Cyclone Risk Mitigation Infrastructure like Multi-Purpose Cyclone 
Shelters, Evacuation/approach Roads/ Bridges, Saline Embankment & Underground Cabling, iii) 

25 
 
Component C: Technical Assistance for Multi-Hazard Risk Management and Capacity building and 
iv) Component D:Project Management and Implementation Support. Components A, C & D are 
fully financedby the Central Government and component B is financed by Central and State 
Government in the ratio of 75:25. The Central Government component is funded through World 
Bank assistance (loan). National Disaster Management Authority is the implementing agency for the 
project.  The project was approved in following two phases as Centrally Sponsored Scheme (CSS): - 
 
(a)  The first phase of NCRMP was approved in January 2011 for the States of Andhra Pradesh & 
Odisha. The project was completed in December 2018 with a total expenditure of ₹ 2524.84 
crores. 
(b)  The second phase of NCRMP was approved in July 2015 for the States of Goa, Gujarat, 
Karnataka, Kerala, Maharashtra and West Bengal at a cost of ₹ 2361.35 crores with 
projectcompletion date as 31stMarch 2020. With subsequent revisions in the light of the 
schemeperformance, ratings, cancellation of non-started activities as well as surrendering of 
savings, the outlay was finally revised to ₹ 1864.38 crores with project completion date as 
March 2023. The grace period for financial closure of the project was up to August 2023. 
The phase II of the project completed with a total expenditure of ₹1806.84 crores. Early 
Warning and Dissemination System (EWDS) has been installed & commissioned in Andhra 
Pradesh, Odisha and Goa. The physical work related to EWDS activity has been completed 
in Karnataka and Kerala and testing for the commissioning of the same is underway. Details 
of physical infrastructure created under NCRMP are as follows: 
 
Table 3.6 
Sub-component Total 
MPCS (No.) 795 
Roads (km) 1291.52 
Bridge (No.) 36 
Saline Embankment (km) 118.18 
Underground Electric Cabling (km) 1387.76 
 
3.9.3 As part of reducing disaster risks and strengthening the capacity of various Government 
departments and communities, training and capacity building is one among the subcomponents of 
the project. 24007 Government officials have been trained through 925 numbers of Capacity 
Building Trainings on various topics as well as 68,988 community representatives have also got 
trained through 3421 Shelter Level Trainings under NCRMP Phase I & II. Apart from that, 795 
Cyclone Shelter Management & Maintenance Committees has also been constituted across 8 project 
States to effectively manage the shelter arrangements. Each committee is comprised of various 
Government officials, community representatives, women & weaker section representatives etc. 
 
3.9.4   The Cyclone Shelters constructed under NCRMP were/are used for various relief and 
rehabilitation purposes during the COVID-19 pandemic and recent cyclones. Other infrastructure 
created under NCRMP were/are being used during various disasters. Further, a Web based Dynamic 
Composite Risk Atlas & Decision Support System (Web DCRA-DSS) tool has been developed 
under NCRMP. This is a real time impact forecast tool to know the location specific cyclone wind 
speed and inundation level due to storm surge, cyclone induced rainfall and riverine flood. This 
information is being used by the States for evacuation planning and response as well as for 
mitigation planning. 
 

26 
 
Observation/Recommendation 
 
3.9.5   Recognizing the absence of NCRMP funding for 2025-26 and the reduced RE for 2024-
25, the Committee recommends prioritizing the sustainment and maintenance of existing 
NCRMP infrastructure to ensure continued benefits from prior investments. The Committee 
recommends the Ministry to explore the possibilities of third phase of NCRMP to extend 
similar cyclone risk mitigation measures to other vulnerable coastal regions not yet covered.

CHAPTER -IV 
 
DEMAND NO. 51 - POLICE 
 
4.1 OVERVIEW 
 
  
4.1.1 The Demand No. 51- Police, contains budgetary provisions for the Central Armed Police 
Forces (CAPFs), Assam Rifles and National Security Guards, Departmental Accounting 
Organization of CAPFs, National Intelligence Grid (NATGRID), Delhi Police, Intelligence Bureau, 
Bureau of Immigration, Narcotics Control Bureau, National Investigation Agency and other Central 
Police Organizations, as well as for certain schemes administered by Ministry of Home Affairs, such 
as Infrastructure projects of Central Armed Police forces (CAPFs) and Delhi Police, Development of 
Border Infrastructure and Management, Mission Mode Project on Immigration, Visa and Foreigners 
Registration & Tracking (IVFRT), Schemes for Safety of Women financed from Nirbhaya Fund, 
Grants to States for raising and maintaining India Reserve Battalions and the Centrally Sponsored 
Schemes viz. Umbrella Scheme of “Modernization of Police Forces” and “Border Area Development 
Programme” “(BADP)”. Provision under this Grant also caters to the needs for Welfare Grants of 
CAPFs, Research, etc. 
 
4.1.2 As far as Police Grant is concerned, the allocations under the ‘Revenue Section’ are 
predominantly for Salaries, Cost of Ration, Travel Expenses and other administrative expenses of 
the CAPFs, CPOs, Delhi Police and other organizations and Grants-in-aid to States/UT 
Governments under various Schemes like ‘Modernization of Police Forces, Border Area 
Development Programme, Schemes financed from Nirbhaya Fund for Safety of Women, etc. 
 
4.1.3 Under the 'Capital Section', the budgetary provisions are for the construction of Residential 
and Office Buildings by the CAPFs and CPOs, procurement of motor vehicles, machinery & 
equipment and creation of infrastructure for securing India's International Borders, viz. Border 
Outposts, Border Roads, Flood Lighting, Fencing,etc. 
 
4.2 CENTRAL ARMED POLICE FORCES (CAPFs) 
 
4.2.1 The provision for Central Armed Police Forces is for defraying expenses relating to 
Establishment charges of Central Armed Police Forces (CAPFs), viz. Central Reserve Police Force, 
National Security Guards, Border Security Force, Indo-Tibetan Border Police, Central Industrial 
Security Force, Assam Rifles and Sashastra Seema Bal. 
 
4.2.2 Assam Rifles, Border Security Force, Indo-Tibetan Border Police and Sashastra Seema Bal 
are known as Border Guarding Forces and have been entrusted with the responsibility of securing 
India's international borders with Myanmar, Bangladesh, Pakistan, China, Nepal and Tibet. National 
Security Guards is an elite security agency specializing in Counter Terrorism operations. Central 
Reserve Police Force is the premier police force deployed in various States and supplements the 
State police forces for general law &order duties andfor maintenance of law and order during 
elections, etc.; its specialized battalions of Rapid Action Force (RAF) are mandated for maintaining 
communal harmony in the country; its commando force, Commando Battalion for Resolute Action 
(COBRA), are deployed for Counter Insurgency and Anti-Naxal operations in LWE affected areas, 
J&K and other places. The Central Industrial Security Force is deployed on security duties at most of 

28 
 
the Airports in the country, offices/factories of Public Sector Undertakings, Central / State 
Government agencies and other installations as decided by the Government of India based on 
security threat assessment. The budgetary provision for CAPFs for the year 2025-26 is given in the 
following table:- 
Table 4.1 
 
Allocation made to CAPFs for the past 3 years  
                                                                                                                                                      (amount in crores of ₹) 
Section Actual 2023-24 BE  2024-25 RE 2024-25 BE 2025-26 
Revenue 93147.48 95010.00 103795.06 107107.98 
Capital 1925.82 1940.12 1852.13 1929.07 
Total 95073.30 96950.12 105647.19 109037.05 
 
4.2.3 The above table shows that the capital allocation for Central Armed Police Forces (CAPFs) 
was initially ₹1940.12 crores in the BE for 2024-25, which was later reduced to ₹1852.13 crores in 
the RE. However, the allocation has been increased again to ₹1929.07 crores in the BE for 2025-26. 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
4.2.4 The Committee recommends that the Ministry may prioritize the timely utilization of 
allocated funds to avoid any underutilization.  Additionally, the Committee suggests that the 
Ministry need to engage in consultations with CAPFs to assess their long-term capital needs 
and develop a multi-year capital expenditure plan to provide greater certainty and 
predictability in funding.  
 
4.3 Representation of Women in CAPFs 
4.3.1 The Committee sought to know about there presentation of women in all CAPFs. The MHA 
has replied that to enhance the representation of women in CAPFs, the Government has implemented 
a policy in January 2016 reserving 33% of constable positions in CRPF and CISF and 14-15% in 
BSF, SSB, and ITBP, specifically for women. This horizontal reservation aims to create a more 
gender-balanced workforce within these crucial security organizations. Recognizing the unique 
challenges and needs of women in these roles, a multi-faceted approach has been adopted to 
encourage their recruitment and retention. 
4.3.2 Several steps have been taken to foster the recruitment of women personnel in CAPF. These 
include extensive publicity campaigns through print and electronic media, waiving application fees 
for female candidates, providing relaxations in Physical Standard Test (PST) and Physical Efficiency 
Test (PET) and extending benefits such as Maternity Leave and Child Care Leave. To ensure fair and 
sensitive recruitment processes, a female member is included on all recruitment boards. Furthermore, 
CAPFs provide creches and Day Care Centers to support women employees with childcare 
responsibilities. Committees have been established at all levels to address sexual harassment 
complaints promptly and effectively. Importantly, women are afforded equal opportunities for career 
progression, including promotion and seniority, in accordance with recruitment rules, at par with 
their male colleagues. Additionally, the appointment of pregnant women is held in abeyance until 
after they have given birth, ensuring their health and well-being. 

29 
 
4.3.3 To improve women's representation in senior leadership positions, direct recruitment to Sub 
Inspector and Assistant Commandant positions occurs through the SSC/UPSC. For appointments to 
remaining senior leadership roles, women are considered for promotions based on inter-se seniority, 
ensuring equality with men. These efforts aim to create a more inclusive and equitable environment 
within the CAPFs and AR, where women can thrive and contribute to the nation's security at all 
levels. 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
4.3.4 The Committee observes the Government's positive steps to enhance women's 
representation in CAPFs, including reservations and supportive initiatives like fee exemptions 
and childcare facilities. However, the Committee notes that actual representation remains 
below target. Therefore, it recommends targeted recruitment drives for women, particularly in 
CRPF and CISF. The Committee emphasizes uniform access to support facilities across all 
units and the importance of robust mechanisms to address workplace issues, including a zero 
tolerance policy against sexual harassment. To further improve women's progression, the 
Committee recommends creating mentorship programs and leadership development 
initiatives. It also suggests exploring flexible deployment options or soft postings during 
specific life stages to enhance retention.  
 
4.4 Cadre Review of CAPFs 
4.4.1 The Ministry has informed the Committee about the Cadre Review of CAPF, which is a 
crucial process for restructuring and optimizing the organizational framework of various cadres 
within the CAPF. This review ensures the elimination of stagnation, the creation of promotional 
opportunities and the enhancement of operational efficiency in alignment with evolving security 
challenges.  
4.4.2 In Border Security Force (BSF), the recent Cadre Reviews in certain BSF cadres have 
yielded positive outcomes by mitigating stagnation, facilitating career progression and strengthening 
the functional effectiveness of the Force. However, the impact of such reviews diminishes over time, 
necessitating periodic reassessment every five years to align with emerging requirements. It may be 
noted that while Cadre Reviews for most BSF cadres are currently under consideration of the MHA 
and the Ministry of Finance (MoF). A matter of particular concern is the Group B & C (General 
Duty) Cadre, which constitutes a significant portion of BSF's workforce. Since the establishment of 
the BSF on 1stDecember, 1965, this cadre has never undergone a review, resulting in prolonged 
stagnation, limited promotional growth and operational inefficiencies, adversely affecting the morale 
of personnel. 
4.4.3 In Sashastra Seema Bal (SSB), the Cadre Review of various SSB cadres between 2022 and 
2024, including Group A Medical Officers Cadre, Group B & C Para-Medical Cadre, Group C 
Tradesmen & Engineering Cadre and Group A, B & C Stenographers Cadre have been approved by 
the Ministry. These recent cadre reviews have effectively helped workforce stagnation, improved 
promotional avenues and enhanced job satisfaction. Notably, a one-level promotion system has been 
introduced for the Tradesmen & Engineering Cadre and a Deputy Commandant (PPS) post has been 
created in the Stenographers Cadre, strengthening the organizational capacity and operational 
efficiency of the force. 

30 
 
In Assam Rifles, the Cadre Review proposal for Group B & C personnel in Assam Rifles is currently 
under process.  
4.4.4 In Central Industrial Security Force (CISF), a Cadre Review was conducted in CISF in 2018 
to enhance career progression and create promotional opportunities across Group B & C posts. As a 
result, rationalization of Group B & C cadres happened and improved the timeline for promotions. 
25 Group A (Executive Cadre) posts (ADG-2, IG-7, DIG-8, and Commandant-8) and six Group A 
(Fire Cadre) posts were sanctioned, leading to better supervisory structures and career 
advancements. 
4.4.5 In Central Reserve Police Force (CRPF), the significant impact of the Cadre Review is 
particularly related to the historic creation of 2,961 Head Constable posts in the Cadre of Cook, 
Water Carrier, Daftry, Peon, Farash, and Safai Karamchari (Ministerial). This decision has not only 
enhanced the dignity and social recognition of force personnel but has also contributed to improved 
operational efficiency and organizational functionality. 
4.4.6 In Indo-Tibetan Border Police (ITBP), a comprehensive Cadre Review for Group A, B & C 
cadres (GD & Non-GD) was approved by the MHA in 2019-20, leading to the creation/upgradation 
of 1,971 posts, improving career progression across various ranks and the abolition of 2,941 
redundant posts, optimizing personnel deployment. Further, in 2023, additional cadre reviews were 
approved, leading to the upgradation of 1,055 posts in Group C Tradesmen & Followers staff, 
facilitating promotion and upgradation of 221 posts in Group B & C Para-Medical cadre, enhancing 
career prospects. 
Observation/ Recommendation 
4.4.7 The Committee appreciates the efforts made by the Ministry in conducting Cadre 
Reviews across various CAPFs to enhance promotional avenues and improve operational 
efficiency. It notes that while some forces, such as SSB, CISF, CRPF and ITBP, have benefited 
from recent Cadre Reviews, others, particularly BSF’s Group-B & C (General Duty) Cadre, 
have not been reviewed since its inception in 1965. The Committee emphasizes that Cadre 
Reviews should be undertaken at regular five-year intervals as their effect diminishes over 
time. It observes that timely Cadre Reviews, as seen in recent cases, have significantly 
contributed to better workforce planning, increased job satisfaction and improved operational 
effectiveness.  
4.4.8 The Committee feels that the MHA along with the Ministry of Finance (MoF) may 
expedite the approval of pending Cadre Reviews, particularly for BSF, Assam Rifles and any 
other forces and to establish a structured mechanism to ensure periodic reviews without 
unnecessary delays. The Committee recommends that financial and structural planning for 
Cadre Reviews be carried out proactively, with dedicated task forces ensuring smooth 
execution. The Committee further recommends that the MHA may explore innovative 
strategies, such as lateral mobility programs, training-based advancements and inter-force 
career progression frameworks, to enhance career growth opportunities across the CAPFs and 
Assam Rifles.  
 
 

31 
 
4.5 Welfare and Rehabilitation Board (WARB) for CAPF 
4.5.1 The Ministry has informed the Committee about various welfare measures undertaken to 
provide financial, educational, and employment support to the families of CAPF personnel killed in 
action, as well as for the rehabilitation of retired personnel. It has highlighted that the Welfare and 
Rehabilitation Board (WARB), established in 2007, oversees the welfare and rehabilitation needs of 
retired CAPF personnel, their familiesand the dependents of deceased personnel, including those 
who are disabled. To facilitate this, 06 Central Welfare Officers (CWOs), 32 State Welfare Officers 
(SWOs) and 156 District Welfare Officers (DWOs) have been deployed across States and UTs to 
provide necessary assistance. 
4.5.2 The Ministry has further informed that financial support is extended through multiple 
schemes, including ex-gratia payments, pensions, gratuity and insurance benefits. Ex-gratia 
payments provide immediate financial relief, while families of deceased personnel receive monthly 
pensions and lump-sum gratuity payments. Additionally, group insurance scheme payments are 
disbursed to the next of kin. Educational support includes scholarships, fee waivers and reservations 
in educational institutions for the children of deceased personnel. Employment opportunities are 
facilitated through compassionate appointments and under the CAPF Punarvaas initiative, the data of 
retired and willing CAPF personnel is made available to private security agencies via the Private 
Security Agency (Regulation) Act (PSARA) website for recruitment. 
4.5.3 The Ministry has also highlighted the Bharat Ke Veer portal, which allows individuals to 
contribute directly to the accounts of the next of kin of deceased personnel or to a dedicated corpus 
fund. Furthermore, a 50% GST subsidy has been introduced for purchases from Kendriya Police 
Kalyan Bhandar (KPKB), with ₹125 crores allocated to support CAPF personnel, retirees and their 
families. The Ministry has informed that additional welfare measures include a 5% quota for 
compassionate appointments, financial aid and free education in CAPF public schools for the 
children of martyrs. The Prime Minister Scholarship Scheme provides ₹30,000 for boys and ₹36,000 
for girls pursuing professional degrees. Other benefits include a ₹33,756 children’s education 
allowance, ₹1,01,256 hostel subsidy and reservation of 26 MBBS and 3 BDS seats for the wards of 
CAPF personnel. 
4.5.4 The Ministry has further informed that ₹50 crores has been sanctioned for ex-gratia lump-
sum compensation to CAPFs in the current financial year. This includes ₹35 lakhs for personnel who 
die in active duty and ₹25 lakhs for those who die on bonafide Government duty. Additionally, 
approval has been granted for the 50% GST subsidy on KPKB purchases from April 1, 2024, to 
further support CAPF personnel and their families. 
Observation / Recommendation 
4.5.5 The Committee appreciates the initiatives taken by the Ministry for the welfare and 
rehabilitation of CAPF personnel and their families. It notes the financial, educational and 
employment support provided, including ex-gratia payments, scholarships, compassionate 
appointments and employment facilitation through the CAPF Punarvaas initiative. The 
Committee observes that these measures play a crucial role in ensuring the well-being of 
serving and retired personnel, as well as the families of those who have laid down their lives in 
the line of duty. However, the Committee emphasizes the need for greater awareness and 
accessibility of these schemes, ensuring that all eligible beneficiaries receive timely assistance. 

32 
 
It recommends the Ministry to strengthen the mechanisms and streamline the process of 
disbursing financial benefits, particularly ex-gratia payments and pensions, to minimize 
delays. The Committee also recommends expanding employment opportunities for the 
dependents of deceased personnel by collaborating with more private-sector entities under 
CAPF Punarvaas. Further, the Committee notes the importance of continuous monitoring and 
evaluation of the Bharat Ke Veer Corpus Fund to ensure transparency and effective utilization 
of contributions.  The Committee notes that the ex-gratia lump-sum payment of ₹35 lakhsfor 
personnel who die in active duty and ₹25 lakhs for those who die on Government duty.   
4.5.6 The Committee further recommends reviewing the current financial allocations to 
welfare schemes, including the 50% GST subsidy on purchases from Kendriya Police Kalyan 
Bhandar, to assess its adequacy and impact. The Committee also emphasizes the importance of 
reserving more seats in professional courses for the children of CAPF personnel and ensuring 
the seamless implementation of the Prime Minister Scholarship Scheme. 
4.6 Housing Facilities and Food Quality for CAPFs 
4.6.1 Recognizing the critical need to improve housing satisfaction among CAPF personnel, which 
stood at 47.95% as of December 2022, the Ministry has initiated several measures to address this 
issue. Forces such as the SSB have reported even lower satisfaction levels, highlighting the urgent 
need for intervention. A significant challenge identified is the unwillingness of personnel to accept 
accommodations in semi-urban or remote areas due to the distance from essential amenities like 
schools and hospitals. To address this, the MHA launched the "CAPF e-AWAS" portal to optimize 
the allocation of existing housing units across different forces. This portal aims to enhance the 
housing satisfaction ratio by allowing personnel from one force to seek accommodations available 
with another. The Ministry has informed that several new houses have been constructed and 
additional projects are currently in pipeline.  
4.6.2. The Ministryinformed the Committee that the mess menu is determined by a representative 
board comprising members from all ranks. This board decides the menu on the basis of instructions 
regarding daily calories intake per day. Food supplies, including rations, dairy products, and 
vegetables, are sourced either from local Army Service Corps (ASC) depots or through monthly 
purchases overseen by a designated board. A separate quality control board ensures the quality of all 
purchased items. To enhance nutritional value, millets have been incorporated into the mess menu. 
Unit doctors conduct regular inspections to ensure that cooks and mess staff maintain strict hygiene 
standards during food preparation. To facilitate feedback and address concerns, monthly mess 
meetings are held, providing a platform for all dining members to voice their opinions. Feedback 
received through these channels is addressed promptly to maintain high standards of food quality, 
hygiene and overall arrangements. 
Observation / Recommendation 
4.6.3 The Committee recommends that the Ministry may develop a strategic roadmap to 
construct housing units for CAPF personnel in urban or well-connected areas to improve the 
current housing satisfaction level. It emphasizes on utilizing rapid construction technologies to 
expedite the process and ensure timely completion of projects. The Committee recommends 
that the "CAPF e-AWAS" portal be enhanced to improve the allocation of existing 
accommodations by ensuring it serves as an accurate inventory of residential houses and 

33 
 
Separated Family Accommodation (SFA) across all CAPFs. The Committee wishes to 
emphasize that achieving a housing satisfaction target of 70-80% in the coming years is 
essential for enhancing living conditions for CAPF personnel and their families. 
4.6.4 The Committee notes with satisfaction the Ministry's explanation of the mess menu 
determination process, highlighting the representative nature of the board responsible for 
menu planning and its adherence to daily caloric intake guidelines. The Committee also 
appreciates the multi-faceted approach regarding food supplies, involving both local Army 
Service Corps (ASC) depots and a designated procurement board. The Committee commends 
the incorporation of millets into the mess menu to enhance nutritional value. Further, the 
Committee acknowledges the importance of regular hygiene inspections by unit doctors and 
the implementation of monthly mess meetings to gather feedback from dining members. The 
Committee recommends the continuation and strengthening of these practices to ensure 
consistently high standards of food quality, hygiene and overall mess arrangements.

bridge the gap at the earliest.

personnel, the number of women personnel remains low. It recommends that the Ministry take 
concrete steps to improve gender representation, such as targeted recruitment drives, better 
incentives and enhanced working conditions for women in CAPFs. The Committee further 
recommends that the Ministry may conduct a periodic workforce assessment to evaluate 
staffing needs and proactively address vacancies. It also suggests improving retention policies, 
welfare measures and career growth opportunities to enhance morale and long-term service 
commitment among personnel. 
4.8 K9 Unit/Canine Squad 
4.8.1 During the meeting of the Committee, a query was raised about the usage of K9 units/canine 
squad in the CAPF. It was informed by the Ministry about the usage of K9 in the CAPF, also known 
as canine squads, for various security and operational tasks. These units are integral to the forces' 
capabilities, providing support in areas such as explosive detection, tracking, and anti-Naxal 
operations. These dogs are trained for tasks like infantry patrol, anti-sabotage sniffer duties and 
detecting bombs and improvised explosive devices (IEDs). K9 units are used in high-risk operations, 
such as counter-terrorism and counter-insurgency missions and play a crucial role in detecting 
explosives and narcotics, helping to prevent attacks and maintain security in sensitive areas. 
Additionally, their ability to navigate challenging terrains and detect hidden threats makes them 
invaluable in border security and surveillance operations. 
4.8.2 K9 units often work alongside other specialized units within CAPF, such as the Rapid Action 
Force (RAF) and Commando Battalion for Resolute Action (CoBRA), to provide comprehensive 
security solutions. Some K9 units, like those in the National Security Guard (NSG), are equipped 
with advanced technology such as two-way audio-video K9 augmentation devices, allowing handlers 

35 
 
to see what the dogs see in real-time, enhancing operational effectiveness. This integration of 
technology not only boosts the efficiency of operations but also ensures the safety of both the 
handlers and the dogs. The training process for these units is rigorous, with handlers undergoing 
extensive training to work effectively with the dogs, ensuring seamless communication and 
operation. The integration of advanced technology and continuous training helps K9 units face 
operational challenges like harsh environments and complex scenarios.  
Observation / Recommendation 
4.8.3  The Committee appreciates the vital contributions of K9 units within the CAPF, as they 
provide indispensable support in explosive detection, tracking, anti-Naxal operations and 
counter-terrorism missions. The Committee also commends the rigorous training of these 
canine squads, which enables them to excel in infantry patrol, anti-sabotage sniffer duties and 
detecting bombs and improvised explosive devices (IEDs), making them crucial in high-risk 
operations. The Committee recommends the formation of more K9 squads, considering the 
exceptional services rendered by these units and their potential to further enhance security 
capabilities.

4.10.1 Delhi Police is primarily responsible for maintaining law and order, prevention of crime and 
providing security to VVIPs/ dignitaries in the National Capital Territory of Delhi. The budgetary 
allocations for the FY 2023-24 under this head have been given below:-

4.10.2  It is clear from table 4.5 that under the Capital Head of Delhi Police, the allocation has

4.10.3  During the meeting of the Committee, Delhi Police has informed that Delhi covers an area of 
1,483 square kilometers. As per the 2011 census, the population was approximately 16.79 million 
(167.88 lakhs), with a significant urban population of about 97.50%. The current population is 
estimated to be around 2.5 crores. Additionally, there is a floating population of approximately 25-30 
lakhs, primarily consisting of individuals seeking employment, business opportunities or visiting for 
tourism and travel purposes. The city has a substantial vehicular population of about 1.28 crores.  
4.10.4  Delhi Police has highlighted that they operates within 2 territorial zones, with 6 territorial 
ranges and 1 transport range. The city is divided into 15 police districts. There are a total of 226 
police stations, including 179 territorial police stations, 15 cyber stations, 16 metro stations, 8 
railway stations, 2 stations at the Indira Gandhi International Airport (IGIA) and 6 special units.  
4.10.5  Delhi Police has also informed the Committee that they have implemented several measures 
to enhance the ease of reporting crimes and complaints. Since 26th January, 2022, e-FIRs have been 
available for house theft and burglary, with over 73,166 cases registered. Additionally, an E-NCR 
facility allows citizens to report stolen items valued under Rs. 5,000. The CCTNS system provides 
SMS updates on FIR registration, arrests and charge sheets. The Crime and Criminal Tracking 
Network and Systems (CCTNS) application has been updated with all 23 patches provided by the 
National Crime Records Bureau (NCRB). To comply with the new laws, e-evidence is being used, 
with over 21,188 electronic evidences uploaded so far. The Integrated Criminal Justice System 
(ICJS) has integrated various e-applications like e-Court, e-Prosecution, e-Forensic and e-Prison. 
Efforts are also underway to strengthen forensic capabilities and enhance K-9 and Bomb Detection 
and Disposal Squad (BDT/BDS) units. They further narrated that in the financial year 2024-25(till 
21/2/2025), they managed a significant number of law and order events. These include 6,547 
demonstrations, 147 processions, 242 meetings, 170 rallies/marches and 3,754 dharnas/strikes or 
other events, totaling 10,860 incidents. Additionally, the police have overseen three major elections, 
which include municipal, parliamentary and assembly elections. This highlights the extensive role of 
Delhi Police in maintaining public order and ensuring the smooth conduct of various events across 
the city. 
Observation/ Recommendation 
4.10.6   The Committee notes the efforts made by Delhi Police in implementing new 
technologies and enhancing public services. It appreciates the successful implementation of e-
FIRs and E-NCR facilities, which have significantly improved the ease of reporting crimes. 
The Committee observes that the reduction in capital allocation could impact infrastructure 

38 
 
development and recommends that efforts be made to enhance capital expenditure to support 
necessary infrastructure improvements. 
4.10.7   The Committee notes that Delhi Police faces a significant challenge with a sanctioned 
strength of 94,257 personnel but an actual strength of 85,690, resulting in a vacancy of about 
8,567 positions. The Committee urges Delhi Police to expedite the recruitment process to fill 
the existing vacancies. The Committee also appreciates the support provided by deploying 
more than ten battalions of CAPFs with the Delhi Police, which to a great extent ameliorate the 
shortage of personnel in Delhi Police. Further, the Committee notes the rising concern of 
crimes against women and suggests more needs to be done to address these crimes effectively. 
The Committee recommends enhancing community awareness through self-defence workshops 
and expanding women-focused programs like Pink Booths and the ‘Himmat’ app. It also 
suggests increasing patrolling in areas deemed unsafe for women and leveraging AI-powered 
surveillance to track suspects in real-time.  
4.10.8   The Committee recommends providing comprehensive training to police personnel on 
the use of weapons and safety equipment.  
4.10.9  The Committee recommends that Delhi Police continue to strengthen its forensic 
capabilities and enhance K-9 and Bomb Detection and Disposal Squad (BDT/BDS) units. It 
suggests further integration of advanced technology into policing, such as AI and data 
analytics, to improve crime prevention and detection. Additionally, the Committee notes the 
importance of public engagement and recommends expanding e-services and community 
outreach programs to foster trust and cooperation between the police and the public. 
4.11 Traffic   Management in Delhi 
4.11.1  Delhi faces significant challenges in traffic management due to its large population of 2.5 
crores and a vehicular population of approximately 1.28 crores, with 1,100 to 1,200 new vehicles 
being registered daily. This high vehicular density contributes to traffic congestion and accidents. 
From 2011 to 2024, the city has experienced a notable number of fatal accidents. To address these 
challenges, Delhi Police has adopted various technological solutions. An automated online challan 
system allows for the issuance of e-challans, online payment, and integration with virtual courts. 
Additionally, automated systems detect red light jumping and over-speeding at multiple locations 
across the city. The Intelligent Traffic Management System (ITMS) is also being developed, 
although its implementation has faced setbacks. The use of technology has led to a significant 
increase in challans issued, with over 19 lakh challans issued in 2024 (till November,24). The budget 
funds shall be utilized for planned activities under various heads for the financial year 2025-26 
which includes modernization of equipment, procurement of vehicles, installation of CCTV cameras 
and implementation of Intelligent Traffic Management System.  Keeping in mind the upgradation of 
security infrastructure such as communication networks and traffic management system, Delhi 
Traffic Police is working on the following major projects/proposals:  
Integrated Traffic Management

awareness campaigns to promote safe driving practices and recommends collaboration with 
other agencies to develop comprehensive traffic management strategies. 
4.12 SAFE CITY PROJECT 
4.12.1 The Safe City Project in Delhi, funded by the Ministry under the Nirbhaya Fund, aims to 
enhance safety for women in public places by leveraging advanced technology. Key components 
include: 
(a) Installation of CCTV Cameras: Over 10,000 CCTV cameras will be installed across Delhi to 
monitor areas frequently visited by women. 
(b) Command and Control Centers: These centers will be equipped with video analytics, AI, 
machine learning, and facial recognition systems at police headquarters, district headquarters, 
and police stations. 
(c) Integration of Data Sets: The project involves integrating 32 data sets and CCTV projects 
under various schemes to enhance surveillance and response capabilities. 
(d) Mobile Command and Control Vehicles: Two vehicles will be deployed to ensure rapid 
response to emergencies.

4.12.2 The Committee notes the ambitious scope of the Safe City Project and appreciates the 
integration of advanced technologies to enhance safety in Delhi. It recommends Delhi Police to 
ensure timely completion of the project and ensure seamless integration of all technological 
components. The Committee suggests conducting regular public awareness campaigns to 
educate citizens about the benefits and usage of these safety measures. Additionally, it urges 
the police to establish a robust feedback mechanism to continuously improve the project's 
effectiveness based on user experiences and crime data analysis.

through awareness programs focused on gender issues and safety measures, ensuring that the 
public is well-informed about the project's objectives and benefits.

4.13.3 On a query about the publication of agitation chapter, it was informed that Data on agitations 
in India was published in the Data on Police Organizations (DoPO) from 2004 to 2017. However, 
after 2017, this chapter was discontinued. The decision was made during a meeting in the Ministry, 
where it was decided that similar data was already being published by the National Crime Records 
Bureau (NCRB) in their report "Crime in India" under the category Offences against Public 
Tranquility. Therefore, it was decided that NCRB would continue to publish this data and BPR&D 
would no longer collect or publish it.
